# PDU Manager LDAP + AI API Upgrade Plan — V3

**Target:** Rack PDU Power Control Center on VM154  
**Current UI:** `http://10.0.20.154:5000/`  
**VM154 host:** `MIAM-00133`  
**Physical PDUs:** `MIAM-00151` (`10.0.20.151`), `MIAM-00152` (`10.0.20.152`), `MIAM-00153` (`10.0.20.153`)  
**Identity service:** LLDAP at `10.0.20.101`  
**LLDAP Web UI:** `http://10.0.20.101:17170/`  
**Implementation model:** GLM 5.3 Flash  
**Status:** Operator decisions incorporated; protected-outlet reboot policy added; ready for implementation discovery

---

# 0. Execution Directive for GLM 5.3 Flash

Your job is to upgrade the **existing** PDU Manager on VM154, not replace it unnecessarily.

The desired result is:

1. Humans log into the PDU Manager with LLDAP credentials.
2. AI agents authenticate with their **long-lived LLDAP username/password** and control outlets through an HTTP API.
3. AI agents no longer need to SSH directly into individual PDUs for normal power operations.
4. Existing outlet protections remain exactly as they are unless the operator explicitly changes them.
5. **Protected outlets have a hard desired steady state of ON.** They may be rebooted/cycled by an authorized administrator, but the PDU Manager must never authorize a protected outlet to remain OFF.
6. Administrative override works from both the web UI and API.
7. Administrative override is granted through revocable LLDAP group membership.
8. Selected AI agents may receive override permission, including permission to reboot protected outlets.
9. The existing local `root` web login remains available as an emergency fallback.
10. The API is reachable from:
   - `10.0.20.0/24`
   - `10.0.10.0/24`
   - the operator's Tailscale network
11. All power-changing operations continue to use VM154's existing PDU backend, including existing locking, confirmation handling, verification, and logging behavior.

**Do not give AI agents the physical PDU SSH credentials.**

**Do not create a generic SSH, shell, or PowerAlert command execution endpoint.** The API must expose only explicit supported PDU operations.

---

# 1. Operator Decisions — Treat These as Requirements

These decisions are already made. Do not ask the operator to choose them again.

## 1.1 Identity

- LLDAP is hosted at `10.0.20.101`.
- The visible LLDAP web interface is on TCP `17170`.
- **Important:** `17170` is the web UI port, not necessarily the LDAP protocol port.
- Discover the actual LDAP listener, Base DN, user DN format, group DN format, and TLS mode from the live LLDAP deployment and/or existing Proxmox LDAP/LLDAP configuration.
- Do not guess the LDAP protocol port and do not change LLDAP settings simply to fit this plan.

## 1.2 API credentials

AI agents will use their existing **long-lived LLDAP username and password**.

For API v1, prefer HTTP Basic Authentication over **HTTPS**:

```http
Authorization: Basic <base64(username:password)>
```

The application must validate those credentials against LLDAP and then obtain group membership for authorization.

Do **not** issue a separate long-lived PDU API key or bearer token in v1.

The browser UI may use an authenticated server-side session after the user submits LLDAP credentials.

## 1.3 Local emergency login

The current local `root` web login must remain available as an emergency fallback.

Requirements:

- preserve the existing local account behavior during migration;
- do not expose the local root credential in API documentation;
- do not place the password in source code;
- store only a secure password hash if the application owns the credential;
- if the current implementation stores plaintext credentials, migrate it to a hash after verifying emergency login still works;
- the emergency local root login should have administrator privileges;
- protected-device override still requires explicit confirmation/reason even for local root;
- API authentication should remain LLDAP-based unless explicitly changed later.

## 1.4 Administrative override

Administrative override must work from:

- browser UI;
- API.

Selected AI agents may perform administrative overrides if they belong to the appropriate LLDAP group.

## 1.5 Protected-outlet steady-state rule

This is a hard safety invariant.

For every outlet that is currently marked `protected` or `critical` in the live PDU Manager:

- normal users/operators must not be able to turn it OFF;
- administrators and AI agents with administrative-override permission **also must not be able to leave it OFF**;
- an authorized administrative override **may REBOOT/CYCLE any protected outlet**;
- a protected-outlet reboot is allowed to enter the OFF state only temporarily as part of one atomic reboot/cycle operation;
- the requested and expected final state of every protected-outlet reboot is **ON**;
- the API must reject a direct `off` action against a protected outlet even when `admin_override=true`;
- this rule applies equally to the browser UI, API, local emergency `root` login, batch actions, and presets;
- no administrative role or override flag may bypass the protected steady-state-ON rule.

For protected outlets, administrative override means **"allow reboot despite protection"**, not **"allow permanent power-off."**

Non-protected outlets may continue to support normal `on`, `off`, and `reboot` actions according to authorization.

---

## 1.6 Network scope

Allow access from:

```text
10.0.20.0/24
10.0.10.0/24
Tailscale / tailnet clients
```

Do not intentionally expose this service to the public Internet.

## 1.7 Protection policy

The live PDU Manager's **current critical/protected flags are the source of truth**.

Before refactoring:

1. export or copy the current protection configuration;
2. compare pre-upgrade and post-upgrade protection flags;
3. preserve them exactly;
4. do not infer new protected status merely from the spreadsheet;
5. do not silently remove protection from any outlet.

## 1.8 First physical test outlet

Use **MIAM-00151 outlet 4** as the proposed first real control test because it appears unassigned in both the current UI and the supplied architecture spreadsheet.

Immediately before a destructive test, verify again that:

- the live UI still labels it as unused/generic;
- no architecture/config record maps a device to it;
- changing it will not affect production equipment.

If any evidence suggests it is now in use, do not test it; choose another operator-approved unused outlet.

---

# 2. Verified Architecture Information from the Supplied Spreadsheet

The supplied server architecture workbook maps:

```text
MIAM-00133 -> MIAM-00153 outlet 9
```

This is a critical self-dependency because VM154 runs on MIAM-00133.

Therefore the canonical PDU configuration must mark:

```yaml
pdu_id: MIAM-00153
outlet: 9
self_host_dependency: true
```

Preserve whatever `protected` / `critical` setting that outlet already has in the existing PDU Manager. If the live application currently marks it protected, it must remain protected.

Additional architecture mappings useful for validation include:

```text
MIAM-00116 OPNsense firewall -> MIAM-00153 outlet 3
MIAM-00170 2.5G switch      -> MIAM-00153 outlet 4
MIAM-00171 10G switch       -> MIAM-00153 outlet 5
MIAM-00120 1G switch        -> MIAM-00153 outlet 6
MIAM-00133 Proxmox host     -> MIAM-00153 outlet 9
MIAM-00172 JetKVM/KVM       -> MIAM-00153 outlet 12
WAN unmanaged switch        -> MIAM-00153 outlet 22
```

These mappings are **context**, not permission changes. The live PDU Manager protection state remains authoritative.

The workbook contains no mapped asset for `MIAM-00151 outlet 4`, which matches the proposed test outlet selection.

---

# 3. Safety Rules

Before editing or testing:

1. Do not power-cycle a real production outlet during discovery.
2. Do not alter PDU firmware or PDU-local authentication.
3. Do not expose PDU SSH credentials to any AI agent.
4. Do not hardcode LDAP passwords, the emergency root password, PDU passwords, or secrets in source code.
5. Do not commit secrets to Git.
6. Make a complete rollback copy before changing the running service.
7. Preserve the existing per-PDU lock / lockout behavior.
8. Preserve the existing OFF/ON/CYCLE confirmation handling.
9. Preserve post-command state verification.
10. Preserve the existing protected/critical outlet configuration.
11. Never let a normal batch action silently include protected outlets.
12. Never expose arbitrary shell or SSH execution via the API.
13. Add idempotency before allowing autonomous AI agents to send destructive requests.
14. Add rate limiting before enabling autonomous destructive control.
15. Use HTTPS before production use of LLDAP passwords through the API.

If live behavior conflicts with this plan, document the conflict and choose the safer behavior rather than guessing.

---

# 4. Phase 0 — Inspect the Existing VM154 Application

Connect to VM154 using the separately supplied administrative credential. Do not copy that credential into implementation documents or Git.

Run read-only discovery first:

```bash
hostnamectl
ip -br addr
ip route
ss -ltnp | grep ':5000' || true
systemctl status pdu-control --no-pager || true
systemctl cat pdu-control || true
ps auxww | grep -iE 'pdu|flask|gunicorn|waitress|python' | grep -v grep
```

Determine:

- application directory;
- service user;
- virtualenv;
- Python version;
- startup command;
- framework;
- config location;
- current local login implementation;
- current protected/critical flag storage;
- PDU SSH credential storage;
- command execution functions;
- command confirmation logic;
- state refresh logic;
- per-PDU lock implementation;
- asynchronous task/job behavior;
- audit/action-log implementation;
- current Markdown configuration import/export behavior;
- any SQLite database already in use;
- reverse proxy/TLS status;
- firewall rules on VM154.

Locate likely files without editing them:

```bash
find /opt /srv /root /home /etc -maxdepth 5 \
  \( -iname '*pdu*' -o -name '*.py' -o -name '*.service' -o -name '*.env' -o -name '*.yaml' -o -name '*.yml' -o -name '*.json' \) \
  2>/dev/null
```

# 5. Back Up Before Modification

Once the live paths are known:

```bash
TS="$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
mkdir -p /root/pdu-control-backups
```

Create:

- full application tarball;
- service unit copy;
- environment/config copy;
- current outlet configuration;
- current protection list;
- dependency list;
- current action log/database copy.

Example only after discovering the actual app path:

```bash
tar -C /ACTUAL/APP/PATH -czf "/root/pdu-control-backups/pdu-control-${TS}.tar.gz" .
systemctl cat pdu-control > "/root/pdu-control-backups/pdu-control.service-${TS}.txt"
```

Also create/confirm a Proxmox VM154 snapshot or backup if operationally available.

**Hard checkpoint:** Do not deploy code changes until rollback artifacts exist.

# 6. Discover the LLDAP Protocol Configuration

The web UI is confirmed at:

```text
http://10.0.20.101:17170/
```

Do not point an LDAP client at port 17170.

Discover the actual LDAP configuration using one or more of:

- the LLDAP container/VM configuration on `10.0.20.101`;
- Docker/Podman compose files if applicable;
- `ss -ltnp` on the LLDAP host;
- LLDAP environment/config files;
- existing Proxmox authentication realm configuration if it already authenticates against this LLDAP instance.

Record in `IMPLEMENTATION-REPORT.md`:

```text
LDAP URI
LDAP protocol port
Base DN
user DN / search filter
group DN / group search filter
bind method
TLS mode
certificate validation behavior
```

Do not reset the LLDAP admin account or modify its database to discover credentials.

If creating groups requires LLDAP administrative credentials that are not already available to the execution environment, stop only at that group-creation step and request the needed LLDAP admin access from the operator.

---

# 7. Create LLDAP Groups

Create these groups unless a naming collision already exists:

```text
pdu-viewer
pdu-operator
pdu-admin
pdu-ai-agent
pdu-ai-admin-override
```

## 7.1 Permission meaning

### `pdu-viewer`

Can:

- authenticate;
- view PDU state;
- view outlet state;
- view appropriate action history.

Cannot change power.

### `pdu-operator`

Includes viewer rights and can:

- turn ON normal outlets;
- turn OFF normal outlets;
- REBOOT/CYCLE normal outlets;
- execute approved batch operations that contain no protected outlets.

### `pdu-admin`

Human/general administrator role. Can:

- perform normal operations;
- use administrative override to REBOOT/CYCLE protected outlets while preserving their required final state of ON;
- view full audit history;
- access PDU Manager administrative configuration functions.

### `pdu-ai-agent`

Marker group identifying an account as an AI/service agent.

This group alone grants no destructive permission.

A normal AI power-control account should normally belong to:

```text
pdu-ai-agent
pdu-operator
```

### `pdu-ai-admin-override`

Grants a selected AI agent the ability to override protected outlets **without making all AI agents administrators**.

For an AI account to override a protected outlet, require:

```text
pdu-ai-agent
AND
pdu-operator
AND
pdu-ai-admin-override
```

This makes override privilege independently revocable by removing only `pdu-ai-admin-override`.

Do not automatically add every AI account to this group.

---

# 8. Central Authorization Model

Implement authorization centrally, not route-by-route.

Conceptually:

```python
can_view(actor)
can_control_normal(actor)
can_override_protected(actor)
can_administer_manager(actor)
```

Suggested behavior:

```text
viewer:
    pdu-viewer OR pdu-operator OR pdu-admin

normal control:
    pdu-operator OR pdu-admin

protected override:
    pdu-admin
    OR
    (pdu-ai-agent AND pdu-operator AND pdu-ai-admin-override)

manager configuration:
    pdu-admin only

local emergency root:
    equivalent to pdu-admin for web UI
```

The application must obtain roles/groups from LLDAP after successful authentication.

Do not authorize by checking specific usernames in source code.

---

# 9. Authentication Design

## 9.1 Browser UI

Use:

```text
username + password login form
        -> validate against LLDAP
        -> load groups
        -> create secure server-side or signed session
```

Session requirements:

- Secure cookie after HTTPS is enabled;
- HttpOnly;
- SameSite;
- inactivity expiration;
- logout support;
- group membership should be refreshed periodically or on new login.

Keep a separate emergency local-root login path or fallback mechanism that does not depend on LLDAP availability.

## 9.2 API

API v1 uses long-lived LLDAP username/password credentials via HTTP Basic Authentication over HTTPS.

Example:

```bash
curl --user "$PDU_LDAP_USER:$PDU_LDAP_PASSWORD" \
  https://10.0.20.154/api/v1/me
```

Do not recommend putting a literal password directly into shell history.

The application may cache a successful username/group lookup briefly for performance, but:

- do not cache the plaintext password;
- keep cache duration short enough that group revocation takes effect promptly;
- administrative override authorization should use fresh or very recent group information.

Recommended initial authorization/group-cache TTL:

```text
60 seconds
```

Make it configurable.

---

# 10. HTTPS and Network Exposure

Because API clients send reusable LLDAP passwords, **HTTPS is mandatory for production operation**.

Preferred topology:

```text
10.0.20/24 clients ----+
                       |
10.0.10/24 clients ----+--> HTTPS reverse proxy on VM154 --> localhost PDU app
                       |
Tailscale clients -----+
```

Use Caddy or Nginx unless an existing reverse proxy already exists.

After cutover, prefer:

```text
PDU Python service -> 127.0.0.1:5000 only
reverse proxy      -> :443
```

Optionally redirect port 80 to HTTPS.

## 10.1 Allowed networks

Permit only the approved management paths:

```text
10.0.20.0/24
10.0.10.0/24
Tailscale traffic
```

For Tailscale, inspect how traffic reaches VM154:

- direct `tailscale0` interface, or;
- routed through the existing subnet router.

Use the observed source/interface behavior rather than blindly allowing a broad public CIDR.

Do not add WAN/public port forwarding.

## 10.2 Certificates

Use the existing internal certificate strategy if one exists.

Otherwise configure a private/internal TLS certificate and document how clients should trust it.

Do not disable TLS verification in production agent examples merely to make `curl` succeed.

---

Both web and API operations must use one backend path:

```text
Browser UI ------------------+
                             |
AI REST API -----------------+--> Authentication
                                   |
                                   v
                              Authorization
                                   |
                                   v
                              Action Service
                                   |
                    +--------------+---------------+
                    |              |               |

                         SSH / PowerAlert menu
                                   |
                       MIAM-00151 / 152 / 153
```

The API must not implement a second independent power-control stack.

Ideal for cluster-wide VMID/IP/name audits.\n  - Login also works as `jordatech@LLDAP-Domain`; use a curl `-c`/`-b` cookie jar (the\n    `__Host-PDMAuthCookie`) instead of hand-built Authorization headers.\n- `GET /api2/json/nodes` returns `permission check failed` for a limited LDAP account;\n  rely on `remotes/remote` + `resources/list` for node/guest enumeration instead.\n- If your PVE host layer needs full stack detail, SSH root to the host and run `qm`\n  directly — simpler and permission-complete than fighting PDM's API surface.\n\n### PVE API as PRIMARY access path (user directive 2026-09-11: \"Switch primary to the PVE API/web path\")\n\nUse the PVE REST API through an LLDAP bot account as the FIRST choice for cluster\noperations; password-SSH runners (noderun.py) drop to fallback for host-only tasks\nthe API can't reach.\n\n- **Bot creds**: staged as a 0600 file (`~/.pve_ldap_bot`: line 1 = username, line 2 =\n  password; realm suffix appended in code). Never echo, never commit.\n- **Auth**: `POST /api2/json/access/ticket` with urlencoded\n  `username=<user>@LLDAP-Domain&password=...` → then send cookie `PVEAuthCookie` on every\n  call + header `CSRFPreventionToken` for non-GET. TLS is self-signed → ssl context with\n  `check_hostname=False, verify_mode=CERT_NONE`.\n- **Ready-made helper**: `scripts/pve_api.py` (login / realms / nodes / resources /\n  permissions / vmconfig <node> <vmid> / vmstatus <node> <vmid> / raw <METHOD> <path> [body];\n  also importable: `from pve_api import api, load_creds`).\n- **Node short-names are INCONSISTENT** — ring nodes are `miam00111/miam00112/miam00143/\n  miam00144` (no hyphen) while `miam-00135`, `miam-00100`, `miam-00149` carry hyphens.\n  NEVER guess a node name in an API path; enumerate `GET /api2/json/nodes` first.\n- **Guest exec via API** (replaces qm guest exec + most SSH-to-VM work):\n  `POST /nodes/<node>/qemu/<vmid>/agent/exec` with body\n  `{\"command\": [\"bash\", \"-lc\", \"echo <b64-of-script> | base64 -d | bash\"]}`, then poll\n  `GET .../agent/exec-status?pid=<pid>` until `exited` (fields: exitcode, out-data,\n  err-data). **PITFALL: urlencode the body with `doseq=True`** — the `command` value is a\n  LIST, and plain urlencode silently DROPS list values, so the POST returns 200-ish while\n  the exec never runs (dispatch returns no pid / 596-looking failures).\n- **VM lifecycle** via `/nodes/<n>/qemu/<id>/status/start` (and stop/reboot) is clean and\n  audited by the bot identity; track completion via `/nodes/<n>/tasks/<upid>/status`.\n- **PDU power portal** (Jordan, 2026-09-11): web-managed PDU portal at\n  `http://10.0.20.154:5000`, root + node-style password staged in `~/.pdu_portal` (600).\n  Control-plane only — power telemetry polling stays on Emporia/GPU NVML per the standing\n  rule (never poll PDU network endpoints .151-.153 for telemetry).\n\n### PVE host/guest shell without sshpass (pexpect) + cloud-init & serial recipes\n\nWhen you must drive Proxmox hosts/VMs from a LAN Hermes box (no web GUI, no existing\ninjected key for the host, possibly no DHCP on the segment, optionally offline hosts),\nthe reusable recipes live in `references/pve-guest-vm-operations.md`, and a ready-made\ndrain-safe password-SSH script runner is `scripts/ssh_run.py` (runs a local .sh on a\nnode, saves output; reads the credential from the profile memory file at runtime and\nnever prints it). Key points:\n- pexpect (user-level `~/ssh-venv`) replaces missing `sshpass`; key-based variant for VMs.\n  Ready-made drain-loop runner: `scripts/ssh_run.py` (see scripts dir; handles the\n  empty/truncated `child.before` pitfall and reads the password from profile memory).\n  **Password auth REQUIRES pexpect** — `subprocess.run(..., input=PW)` fails silently\n  (Permission denied) because the password prompt appears AFTER stdin is consumed.\n  If `sshpass` is absent and uninstalled, port the loop to pexpect, never to input-feed.\n- ssh_run.py takes **IP addresses or full resolvable names, not bare node short-names**\n  (`miam00112` → \"Could not resolve hostname\"; use `10.0.20.112`).\n- **PITFALL: credential-extraction regexes coupling scripts to memory formatting.**\n  ssh_run-style scripts that parse the profile MEMORY.md for the password (`r\"root/(\\S+?)\\)\"`\n  etc.) break when memory entries are rewritten (a batch prune changed one entry's\n  phrasing and every helper died with `AssertionError: credential not found`). Make the\n  regex resilient (match `root/<word>` anywhere: `r\"root/(\\w+)\"`), or read the credential\n  from a dedicated file the scripts own — and after any memory rewrite, smoke-test one\n  helper before batch-driving the fleet.\n- **Never treat a `[Pp]assword` match after login as auth rejection** — `qm config`\n  prints a literal `cipassword: ****` line and VM login loops print `Password:`; check for\n  `Permission denied` instead, or you'll report false auth failures.\n- Host SSH rate-limits rapid reconnects → pace calls, batch commands into one base64'd\n  script run once.\n- **PITFALL: pexpect `child.before` empty/truncated on long batched output.** A single\n  `expect(EOF)` + one `child.before` read can return EMPTY (match at buffer start) or\n  lose most output. Drain instead: loop\n  `expect([r'[Pp]assword:', pexpect.EOF, pexpect.TIMEOUT])` until EOF, appending\n  `child.before` on every iteration, then `\"\".join()` the pieces. Keep the password\n  pattern inside the loop so late prompts still match; treat success as the absence of\n  `Permission denied` in the joined text.\n- Large binaries to offline hosts: serve via `python3 -m http.server` on the internet-\n  capable LAN host, `curl` inside the Proxmox host. pexpect-scp with `encoding='utf-8'`\n  corrupts binaries.\n\n### `qm guest exec` / `pct exec` / `pct push` only work on the OWNING node\n\n`/etc/pve` is cluster-readable, but guest-exec commands resolve the guest config against\nthe LOCAL node. Running `qm guest exec 103` from a node that doesn't own VM103 fails with\n`Configuration file 'nodes/<wrong-node>/qemu-server/103.conf' does not exist`. SSH to the\nowning node first (fleet map: VM103→miam-00111, VM401→miam-00112, VM109→miam-00143,\nVM111→miam-00144; CT115→miam-00147). `pct push` additionally requires the CT to be running.\n\n**PITFALL: `qm guest exec <vmid> -- bash -s` does NOT forward stdin.** A heredoc piped at\n`qm guest exec` feeds the node-side qm process, not the guest — the call returns\n`{\"exitcode\":0}` with EMPTY `out-data` while the guest script never ran. This silently\nno-ops entire batch installs. The reliable pattern is base64-in-command:\n`qm guest exec <vmid> -- bash -c \"echo <base64> | base64 -d | bash\"`.\nSame class of confusion: `scp node:/tmp/x` lands the file on the NODE — to move a file\nnode→CT use `pct push <ctid> /tmp/x /tmp/x` (and `pct pull` to fetch out).\n\n### Inspecting the GUEST vs the NODE (verify your vantage point)\n\nA script run directly through SSH lands on the NODE shell — its output (`pve-root` df,\nQEMU process list, host `lspci`, missing `nvidia-smi`) describes the hypervisor, NOT the\nguest. This burned a full diagnostic round: a \"bare guest\" verdict was drawn from node-shell\noutput while the guest was actually fully built (drivers + venv + model cache intact).\nRule: every guest-internal probe (cloud-init status, nvidia-smi, venv/model dirs, guest FS\nusage, guest internet) MUST be wrapped in `qm guest exec <vmid> -- bash -lc '...'` on the\nowning node. Before mutating anything based on an inspection, state which vantage point\nproduced the evidence.\n\n### Deploying a file INTO a VM guest when your box has no direct key\n\n1. base64 the deploy script locally; run it to the owning node over SSH, decode to /tmp there.\n2. From the node: `scp /tmp/x.sh <guest-user>@<guest-ip>:/tmp/` — the node holds the SSH\n   keypair baked into the guest's cloud-init `authorized_keys` (e.g. the `root@miam-00135`\n   RSA key referenced in cloud-config users).\n3. From the node: `ssh <guest-user>@<guest-ip> \"sudo bash /tmp/deploy.sh\"` — the cloud-init\n   user has NOPASSWD sudo. Direct SSH from your own box only works if YOUR key is in the\n   guest's authorized_keys; otherwise this node-relay path is the reliable route.\n\n### Debian 12 LXCs ship no sudo\n\n`sudo: command not found` inside a fresh Debian 12 CT. As root use\n`runuser -u <user> -- <cmd>` (e.g. `runuser -u postgres -- psql ...`). Locale/UTF-8 perl\nwarnings in these CTs are harmless noise.\n\n### Reconstructing an undocumented service into systemd from bash_history\n\nWhen a guest runs a critical service (e.g. vLLM) with no unit file, recover the final\nbattle-tested invocation from `grep -ihE '<service>' /root/.bash_history /home/*/.bash_history`\n—the LAST occurrence is the current working config. Turn it into\n`/etc/systemd/system/<svc>.service` (Environment lines for exports like HF_HOME, ExecStart\nwith all flags, Restart=on-failure, TimeoutStartSec generous for model load), then\n`daemon-reload && enable --now`, and verify via the service's HTTP endpoint\n(`/v1/models` + a small chat completion for vLLM) before calling it up.\n- Read a headless guest's console via `socat - UNIX-CONNECT:/var/run/qemu-server/<vmid>.serial0`\n  under `pty.fork()` — the only view before the guest agent/network are up. Verified\n  working pattern: `pty.fork()` + `os.execvp('socat', ...)`, write `\\n`, then\n  `select()`-loop reads for ~20s; a booting/idle guest yields the `login:` banner.\n  Wrap it in a script landed on the node via base64-heredoc, then `python3 <script>`\n  — nodes have no pexpect installed (stdlib `pty`/`select` only).\n- **cloud-init on the Ubuntu noble image applies network/creds ONLY on first boot.**\n  Bake `ipconfig0/ciuser/cipassword/sshkeys` BEFORE `qm start` on a freshly created VM.\n  Patching a booted VM then `qm reboot` does NOT re-apply them.\n  ### Re-run WITHOUT recreating: bump the cloud-init instance-id\n  When a first boot half-applied (ssh/packages missing, wrong IP) you do NOT need to\n  destroy/rebuild: edit the user snippet (`/var/lib/vz/snippets/<name>.yaml`), bump\n  `instance-id` in the meta snippet (e.g. `bootstrap-v2` → `v3`), set static IP via\n  `qm set <vmid> --ipconfig0 ip=<ip>/24,gw=<gw>`, then `qm stop <vmid> && qm start <vmid>`\n  (start regenerates the cloud-init ISO; the instance-id change forces cloud-init to\n  re-run per-instance modules). Poll readiness with `timeout 2 bash -c 'echo > /dev/tcp/<ip>/22'`\n  + `qm agent <vmid> ping`, then verify `cloud-init status --long` = done/no errors.\n  ### Static vs DHCP on vmbr0: VERIFY before asserting\n  Do NOT assume the LAN lacks DHCP — verified 2026-09 this vmbr0 /24 HAS a DHCP\n  server (a cloud-init `ip=dhcp` VM leased an address; all Hermes LXCs run `ip=dhcp`).\n  **After any build assigns statics, run the full audit in\n  `references/network-ip-collision-audit.md`** (asset-sheet MAC cross-check + ARP\n  classification + OPNsense DHCP pool boundary check via config.xml download) — the\n  user explicitly asks for this double-check, and it caught the corosync collision\n  below plus stale .115 references left in app configs.\n  Still assign STATIC IPs to infrastructure roles (managers/gateways/DB) so identities\n  never drift, but prove an address is free before assigning:\n  1) `qm list`/`pct list` per node + `pvesh get /cluster/nextid`\n  2) `grep -rE '10\\.0\\.20\\.[0-9]+' /etc/pve/qemu-server/ /etc/pve/lxc/`\n  3) `ip neigh show` + parallel ping sweep of the /24\n  4) **CLUSTER-WIDE: also check `/etc/pve/corosync.conf` `ring0_addr` values** — bare-metal\n  cluster nodes own LAN IPs too. A CT was provisioned on an IP that was actually cluster node\n  `miam-00115`'s corosync address; ARP resolved (node answers), ping worked, but the port\n  refused because the wrong host owned the IP. Signature: port REFUSED (not timeout) while\n  the service is verified listening on the CT itself → suspect IP collision, compare\n  `ip neigh show <ip>` MAC against the CT's real NIC MAC (`pct exec <ct> -- ip link show eth0`).\n  A failed probe alone does NOT prove unused (host may be down); abort on any\n  contradictory signal. **USER RULE (2026-09): \"Use DHCP if you are confused instead of\n  static IP assignment\"** — when free-space evidence is ambiguous or contradictory, lease\n  dynamically rather than gamble on a static; run the audit afterwards to see what was\n  leased. **USER RULE (2026-09-10): ping sweeps + ARP + lease tables are NOT sufficient —\n  the corosync `ring0_addr` map is the FIRST check, because it includes POWERED-OFF nodes\n  (a ping sweep missed offline MIAM-00110 @ 10.0.20.110 and Jordan had to correct the\n  assignment: \"10.0.20.110 is definitely used as a static IP for MIAM-00110\").** Corollary:\n  node names follow `MIAM-00XXX → 10.0.20.XXX` — treat the whole node-number band as\n  reserved for bare metal even when a node is offline. The OPNsense Kea API (leases +\n  static reservations, see `references/opnsense-kea-dhcp.md`) plus the corosync map\n  together are the complete claimant set; prefer addresses verified against BOTH. To find a guest's IP read-only:\n  `qm guest cmd <vmid> network-get-interfaces | grep -A2 '\"ip-address\"'`.\n  After re-IPing a guest, GREP-AND-REPOINT every consumer config that referenced the\n  old IP (app code, gateway config, DSN/secret files) — an old address survived\n  `sed`-able configs after the CT115 move and was only caught during the collision audit.\n\n## NVIDIA GPU passthrough + driver qualification (GDI)\n\nUse for passing NVIDIA GPUs to Proxmox VMs and qualifying the NVIDIA driver (R580 Open, etc.)\nthrough the GPU Driver Installer v4.1 controller — the disciplined checkpoint → plan → install →\narm → validate sequence, one canary GPU at a time. This is the pattern JIT-vm-gpu nodes use to\nreproduce the reference build on fresh hardware.\n\nKey points (full recipe in `references/gdi-gpu-driver-qualification.md`):\n- Controller VM serves HTTPS `:8443` (Basic Auth); each host runs a forced-command `gdi-node`\n  endpoint reading request JSON from STDIN. `inventory` returning `{\"ok\":true}` is the\n  enrollment smoke test.\n- Safety gates live in `/etc/gdi-node.json`: `hardware_cleared`, `watchdogs_suspe

### [2026-09-11T03:19:45.990877+00:00] assistant

**Critical §14.2 finding confirmed:** the driver's `reboot` already maps to the PDU-native atomic **"4- Cycle Load"** PowerAlert menu operation (PDU firmware commits the cycle itself after the y+ENTER confirmation — proven by the driver's own design of closing the session and verifying via a *new* SSH session). For the self-host case (153:9), I must skip post-cycle verification (VM154 will be down) and reconcile after return — matching §14.4 exactly.

Now reading the app orchestration layer:
